- [x] The headline weight looked broken because it never said it was an
  average (3 Sep 2026). Ashley logged 85kg and saw 86.0, logged 85kg again and
  saw 85.7, and reported the weight display as wrong. The maths was right: the
  headline is the 7-day rolling average VISION-ARCHITECTURE.md §5.4 requires
  ("never a raw daily reading as the headline"), and her onboarding weight of
  87 is one of the readings feeding it — (87+85)/2 = 86.0, then
  (87+85+85)/3 = 85.7. Both figures she saw were exactly correct.
  THE DEFECT WAS COMMUNICATION, not computation: a big unlabelled number with
  "85.0 kg today" a few centimetres below it, two numbers for one weight and
  neither explaining itself. Reading that as a bug is the right reading of
  what was on screen. Asked with the alternative on the table (show the raw
  reading as the headline instead, overriding the vision doc); she chose to
  keep the smoothing and label it. Now renders "7-day average" beside the
  number, always — not gated on sample count, since the confusion does not
  resolve once a third weigh-in arrives.
  MY OWN GATE FAILED FIRST, and it is the more useful half of this entry: the
  check sliced the JSX block and tested it for /average/i, and the long
  explanatory COMMENT I had just written above the label satisfied it. Both
  mutations — label deleted, label hidden behind the thin-sample condition —
  passed against a screen with no label at all. A check its own subject's
  comment can satisfy is not a check. Rewritten to strip comments and match a
  RENDERED text node between > and <; both mutations now red. Found only by
  running the mutations, not by reading the check.

- [x] **I SHIPPED A BLACK SCREEN TO PRODUCTION (2 Sep 2026).** The chat-tab
  attention effect in #15 was written under handleTabChange — a few lines
  before App.tsx's JSX, and ~70 lines AFTER its `if (!profile)` early return.
  First render: profile is null, App returns early, the effect never runs.
  Profile resolves: the render passes the return and it does. One more hook
  than the previous render is precisely what React forbids — "Rendered more
  hooks than during the previous render" unmounted the whole tree, and EVERY
  user got a black screen on load, because every session starts with a null
  profile. Fixed by relocating the effect beside its own two useState calls,
  above the return.
  WHY EVERY GATE STAYED GREEN, which is the part worth keeping: tsc and the
  bundler do not check hook ordering; test:coach-opener asserted the effect's
  CONTENT and not its POSITION; and nothing in the suite renders App. I wrote
  "NOT VERIFIED IN A BROWSER from here" in this file's own entry for #15 and
  merged anyway — the flag was pointing straight at this and I treated
  "build passes, gates green" as enough for a change to the app's root
  component. Ashley found it on her phone.
  GUARD: test:coach-opener §7 now reads App.tsx and asserts NO hook call
  (useState/useEffect/useRef/useCallback/useMemo/useLayoutEffect/useReducer/
  useContext) appears after that early return — the class, not the instance.
  Mutation: put the effect back where it broke production -> red, naming the
  line. With every hook above the only early return the sequence is identical
  on every render, which is a stronger guarantee than any single browser run.
  LOCAL RENDER: the built app boots and React mounts (headless Chromium,
  no hook error) but stops at the auth screen, since the sandbox cannot reach
  Supabase — so it could NOT exercise the null-to-set profile transition.
  Stated rather than dressed up as end-to-end proof.

- [x] Chest got 8 hard sets a week where back got 12 (2 Sep 2026). Third item
  from the research document Ashley shared, and the first thing to come out of
  counting sets per muscle — something the app had never done. The existing
  balancer balances PATTERNS (push sets vs pull sets) and does it right, but a
  push set splits across chest/shoulders/triceps while a pull set concentrates
  on back/biceps, so push:pull sits in band while the muscles underneath are
  lopsided. Verified on a live week (14 chest sets vs 22 back) before trusting
  the aggregate. She chose "even out chest against back". Built as a second
  pass in enforceWeeklyPatternBalance aiming at a 1.25 band, preferring to move
  a set from shoulders (the biggest surplus, 15.3/wk) to chest. muscleGroupsOf
  now lives in exercise-db.ts so the generator and the report script cannot
  drift; erectors are their own group, not back. Measured: chest 7.9 -> 8.6
  mean, weeks under 10 75.1% -> 69.2%, back:chest 2.00 -> 1.72, push:pull
  weeks out of band 42 -> 42 (unchanged). Gate test:muscle-balance, four
  mutations red. Plan doc: docs/plans/chest-against-back.md.
  COST, stated: test:quality 11.54 -> 11.51 (0 below floor). Time fit and goal
  alignment each gave up a little because added sets cost minutes. Unlike the
  other two changes today this one is not free.
  NAMED RESIDUAL: it reaches 1.72, not the 1.25 it aims at. Chest often has
  only one or two adjustable accessory slots and once each is at its role
  ceiling the only move left is adding a whole chest EXERCISE, which post-
  periodization needs a rebuild the code deliberately does not do. Separate
  change, not a budget.

- [x] Four in five main lifts rested under two minutes (2 Sep 2026). Second
  item taken from the research document Ashley shared: short rests cost real
  work, and the loss lands on the hardest lift of the day. Measured across the
  9,216-plan grid before asking her: 147,897 of 182,847 main-lift slots (80.9%)
  under 120s, most commonly 90s / 60s / 75s; the trimmer reached a tier-1 main
  lift 14,764 times and landed 4,269 of them on the old 60s floor. Cost, also
  measured before asking (whole grid re-swept at 120s): mean exercises per
  session 7.57 -> 7.46, about one fewer every nine sessions. She chose "two
  minutes, but conditioning keeps its short rest", so minLoadedMainLiftRestSeconds
  is now 120 on hypertrophy/fat_loss/functional and stays 90 on conditioning —
  a value change, not a new mechanism, because that field already WAS this rule.
  quality-score's main_lift_short_rest moved off its hardcoded 60 to the goal's
  own floor, and now fires 0 times across the grid. test:audit 17,423 / 0;
  test:quality 11.54 / 12 (unchanged), 0 below the floor. Gate
  test:block-rest-sizing §6 pins her ruling BY NAME plus an end-to-end
  assertion; three mutations red. Plan doc: docs/plans/two-minutes-on-the-big-lift.md.
  METRIC CHANGE: main_lift_short_rest can now fire between 60 and 120s on a
  loaded main lift, so its prior counts are not comparable.
  FLAGGED FOR ASHLEY, NOT DECIDED: her ruling is goal-scoped, so a hypertrophy
  trainee's Metabolic Conditioning BLOCK now rests two minutes on its loaded
  main lift despite that block promising "short rest, sustained output". One
  line to change if she wants the exception widened to the phase.

- [x] **THE COACH QUOTED A WEIGHT THE PLAN DID NOT PRESCRIBE — AND IT WAS MY OWN REGRESSION, HOURS OLD.** `test:coach-plan-context` went red with `Backpack Curl … value 8 … sentAs " @ ~7.5kg"`. A prescribed weight is **three fields** — `suggested_load_kg` (the number the audit reads), `suggested_load` (the string every screen and the coach's prompt read) and `per_set_load` (the set grid you tick off) — and the rep-target load floor I built the same day raised the NUMBER by hand and left the other two on the old figure. So the plan held 8kg while the Exercise tab and the coach both said ~7.5kg: not a rounding difference, two different answers to "what am I lifting today?". **61 loaded exercises across a 19,724-exercise sweep → 0.** 42 floor applications produced those 61 — the weekly rebuild's carry-forward fallback propagates a stale label into later weeks. Fixed by routing the floor through `rebuildLoadForExercise`, the one function that writes all three together; its doc comment claimed it only ever pulled loads DOWN, which stopped being true the moment the floor called it, so that was corrected too.
- [x] **"ONE LIFT, ONE WEIGHT, IN A GIVEN WEEK" WAS A COMMENT, NOT A RULE.** `weightDecidedThisWeek` states it and states the tie-break — *"the LOWER one wins: never tell someone to lift more than the lift has earned elsewhere in the same week"* — but it is consulted while each slot is being BUILT, so it can pull a later day down to an earlier one and has nothing to pull an earlier day down *with*. **When the heavier day came first, the rule silently did not apply.** Measured on the audit's own grid: `Shoulder Press Machine` week 3, intermediate/100kg/full_gym — Monday **45kg**, Wednesday **32.5kg**, identical 3×11-13 @ RPE 6-7. `enforceOneWeightPerPrescription` now settles the finished week before the per-day safety ceilings run. **92 divergences → 0**, `test:audit` unchanged at 17,423 / 0.
- [x] **AND THAT GATE'S OWN EXEMPTION HAD BEEN COVERING FOR IT.** `test:week-load-consistency` §2 waved through 87 of those 92 as legitimate per-day safety ceilings and ratcheted the rest at 96. If a ceiling had really been diverging them it would have diverged them again after the equalisation — it did not, so the exemption was matching the right SHAPE for the wrong reason. Both the exemption and the ratchet are deleted rather than loosened. **The 202 → 96 → 0 history is not one curve**: the first two were counted with the exemption applied, this one without.
- [x] **THE MUTATION THAT SURVIVED EVERYTHING, WHICH IS THE FINDING WORTH KEEPING.** Swapping `Math.min` for `Math.max` in the new pass — equalising a week UP instead of down, i.e. prescribing more weight than the lift earned — left **every gate in the repo green**, `test:audit`'s 17,423 combinations included. §2 can prove the two days AGREE; it cannot see WHICH of them moved, and the direction is the only thing that makes the pass safe. The function is exported for exactly one reason: §4 calls it directly with a two-day fixture and pins that the heavy day comes down, in both orders, taking its label and set breakdown with it.
- [x] **THE BROWSE REDESIGN HAND-ROLLED A KG STRING, AND `test:load-display` WAS RED ON `main` SAYING SO.** `ProgramBrowse.tsx`'s main-lift line printed `` `~${ex.suggested_load_kg}kg` ``. `loadingMode` prices anything dumbbell-capable **per hand** — 47.8% of prescriptions — so that line sat one tap above `~14kg per hand` inside the expanded day and read as a third of a lift that is two-thirds of it. This is the exact defect that gate exists for and it named the file; mine, from #12. Now uses the plan's own string.
- [x] **`onboarding-chat` HAS BEEN UNDEPLOYABLE FOR TWO DAYS, AND A GATE SAID IT WAS FINE.** Ashley's deploy failed with `Failed to bundle the function (reason: The module's source code could not be parsed: Expected ',', got '{' at .../onboarding-chat/index.ts:10:8)`. The cause is one line: `c2a48bb` (30 Aug, "a ceiling on what the AI functions can cost") inserted its new import **inside another import's braces** —

  ```
  import {
  import { checkSpendCap, ONBOARDING_CAP } from '../_shared/spend-cap.ts';
    callsOf,
    ...
  } from "./reply-resolver.ts";
  ```

  **THE CONSEQUENCE IS THE SPEND CAP ITSELF.** That commit's whole purpose was to stop the AI endpoints running up an unbounded bill on a key that ships inside the app's JavaScript. It reached three of the four functions. `onboarding-chat` has not been deployable since the commit that added it, so **production's onboarding endpoint has been running with no cost ceiling the entire time** — and the failed deploy left the old version live, so nothing looked broken.
  **AND `test:spend-cap` PASSED THROUGHOUT.** It scans source text for `checkSpendCap(req, ONBOARDING_CAP, ...)`, and that string is there. The gate is not wrong; it answers *"was this written?"* while the question that mattered was *"can this ship?"*. A gate reading a file the deployer cannot parse is this repo's check-satisfied-by-the-wrong-thing shape wearing the compiler's clothes.
  **NOTHING IN 120 GATES COULD HAVE SEEN IT.** `npx tsc -b` covers `src/` only — the edge functions are Deno modules with `.ts` specifiers and `jsr:` URLs, deliberately outside the app's tsconfig. The one artifact this repo hands to a live server had no syntax check of any kind.
  **AND THE GATE'S FIRST RUN ON ASHLEY'S MACHINE WENT RED AGAINST A CORRECT REPO — my bug, one line.** Its path helper was `p.replace(FUNCTIONS + '/', '')`, a hardcoded forward slash. On Windows `join` produces backslashes, so the prefix never matched, the helper returned the full path, and §1's `startsWith('_shared/')` failed — in the same run where the deploy it was guarding succeeded. That is the shape `test-frozen-weeks` already warns about in as many words: *a check that fires on a legitimate difference is worse than no check, because the next person reads past it.* Now `relative()` does the stripping and the separators are normalised, so comparisons and reported paths read identically on both platforms; proven against Windows-style paths directly, since this sandbox is Linux and cannot reproduce it. The nested-import mutation still turns the gate red, so the fix did not soften it. **Worth noting for the next gate:** seven other scripts use the same `replace(ROOT + '/', '')` idiom, but all seven use it only to build a REPORTED string — ugly paths on Windows, never a false failure. Mine was the only one using it inside a condition.
  Fixed, and gated: **`test:functions-deployable`** parses every file under `supabase/functions/` with the TypeScript parser (syntax only — no types, no network, exactly the layer that failed) and checks that every relative import resolves to a file on disk. Those are the two ways `supabase functions deploy` refuses a bundle. Both mutations turn it red: reintroduce the nested import, and point one import at a filename nobody wrote. **Still needs `npm run deploy:functions:prod -- onboarding-chat` to actually reach production.**

- [ ] **A PRODUCTION DEPLOY THAT SHIPPED THE OLD CODE AND LOOKED LIKE A SUCCESS, 1 Sep 2026.** Ashley ran `npm run deploy:functions:prod -- chat-gemini` and it reported `Deployed Functions on project sdkhuczcfnqqimdgfiks`. Correct project, real upload, and **the wrong bytes**: her local clone was at `30f1594` (#10), three merges behind `origin/main` (`be01d77`), so what went to production was the `chat-gemini` from before #11 and #12. The fix that stops *"I want to work out today"* rebuilding sixteen weeks of her plan — the one she hit herself — was in that gap and is **still not live**. In the same session `npm run db:push-both` said *"Remote database is up to date"* for the same reason: `20260902120000_add_start_preference_to_fitness_profiles.sql` does not exist on her disk, so the CLI had nothing to push.
  **RESOLVED 1 Sep 2026, and verified from the output rather than assumed.** Ashley pulled to `be01d77` and re-ran. `db:push-both` this time NAMED the migration — *"Applying migration 20260902120000_add_start_preference_to_fitness_profiles.sql"* on TEST and again on PRODUCTION — which is the proof the earlier "up to date" could not give. `chat-gemini` redeployed from the current checkout, so the "I want to work out today" fix is finally live. `onboarding-chat` failed on the same run for an unrelated reason — see the entry above.
  **THE LESSON IS ABOUT THE OUTPUT, NOT THE MISTAKE.** Both commands print a success that is indistinguishable from a no-op caused by a stale checkout. A deploy names the project it hit and never the commit it shipped; a migration push says "up to date" whether the file is applied or absent. Two of the three migrations WERE genuinely applied and one was not, from the identical message. **Worth building: have `deploy-functions.mjs` and `db-push-both.mjs` print the current `git rev-parse --short HEAD` and warn when `git fetch --dry-run` shows the branch behind its remote.** That is mechanical and would have caught this before the deploy ran, not after.

- [x] **THE APP HAD TWO BRAKES AND NO ACCELERATOR — VISION STEP 4'S MISSING HALF, BUILT.** Ashley picked "make the plan learn from you" when asked what was next. Investigating narrowed it hard, and **I nearly built a second copy of something that already exists**: `block-consistency.ts` (Step 5) already holds volume when attendance drops, with her own rulings in it. Together with `block-review.ts` (Step 4, holds a stalled lift's weight flat) that is two thirds of *"it should also read backward"* — and **both can only slow someone down.** Nothing could speed them up: beat the prescription for four weeks and the next block still started from the formula's guess, because `getDoubleProgressionRecommendation`'s "go up next time" note is DISPLAY ONLY and was never written back.
  `beat-target-offer.ts` closes it. **Ashley's ruling, asked directly: propose it, she confirms** — and the asymmetry with the brake is principled, not accidental. block-review APPLIES its hold directly because a decline there would mean adding weight to someone genuinely stalled (her own earlier correction); this OFFERS because a decline here just means not adding weight to someone who earned it. The brake may act alone; the accelerator asks first. An automatic version was rejected in the original design doc for "encouraging chasing reps past good form", and confirm removes that without losing the feature.
  **NO MIGRATION, and that was the one open implementation question.** The offer lives on the existing `pending_actions` rail: `createPendingAction` is client-callable, `kind` is free-form, and the partial unique index on `(profile_id, scope_key)` for open rows gives "offered once per block per lift" **enforced by the database** rather than by a flag. Decline is a row status. Surfaced through `adaptationMessages`, the same rail the weight-basis offer uses.
  **IT REUSES THE BRAKE'S DEFINITION OF PROGRESS RATHER THAN INVENTING ONE.** `beatTargetInBlock` is `didExerciseStallInBlock` inverted, plus the same `MIN_SESSIONS_TO_JUDGE` bar — two definitions of "progress" in one app is how the accelerator and the brake end up disagreeing about the same four weeks. The length check is NOT redundant with it: the stall check returns `false` below three sessions meaning *"not enough data"*, so a bare `!stalled` would read two lucky sessions as evidence. Confirm rebuilds through `prescribeLoad(..., forceStartingWeightKg)` — the same door block-review uses — so number, display string and per-set breakdown come from one calculation and cannot disagree, which is the bug fixed earlier today and must not be reintroduced. A `claimPendingAction` precondition re-reads the planned weight, so a rebuild or swap between the offer and the tap refuses rather than writing a number nobody agreed to.
  Gate `test:beat-target` weights its cases to the unsafe direction: §2 (stays quiet) has more cases than §1 (fires), and §3 sweeps 48 planned/lifted combinations to prove it can never propose downward — which would make the accelerator a silent brake bypassing block-review's evidence bar. Audit **17,423 / 0 unchanged**; that proves the GENERATOR did not regress, not that it validated the offer, which is DB-backed and proven by its own gate.
- [x] **AND TWO OF MY OWN GATE CHECKS WERE SATISFIABLE BY THE WRONG THING — CAUGHT BY MUTATING THEM, NOT BY READING THEM.** Five mutations were run; three failed immediately and **two sailed straight through**. `/if \(patched\) \{/` matched the *weight-basis* handler elsewhere in `App.tsx`, so deleting the guard from the catch-up handler left the gate green. `/plannedKgForSlot\(/` matched a dead reference left behind when the precondition was replaced with `async () => true`. Both are the exact defect class this repo keeps finding in its own gates, written by me, into a file whose header says the checks are weighted toward safety. Both are now scoped to their own subject — the claim call and the named handler body — with a sanity check that the slice is non-empty, and both mutations turn them red. **A mutation that survives is the only reason either was found.**

- [x] **AND THE HEADING WAS WRONG TWICE IN ONE DAY, IN THE SAME DIRECTION.** #13 shipped `Block 4: Hypertrophy` as the heading. That named the block — and **still repeated for four weeks**, which was Ashley's actual complaint. She sent a mockup with no message, confirmed it was a design to build, and ruled: **lead with the week.** The heading is now the week's role (Baseline / Building / Peak / Deload), a different word every week; the block is named in full on the line beneath as `Block 4 of 4 · Hypertrophy · weeks 13–16`; and the sixteen-tick strip came back with the four block NAMES under it, replacing both the block rail and the four-segment week strip that shipped hours earlier.
  **THREE THINGS IN THE MOCKUP WERE NOT COPIED, and saying why is the point.** (1) Its title read `Peak` while its week bar read `Week 13 · Baseline` — the same week, two different answers, and Baseline is the correct one. Once the heading carries the role the two would be the same word twice within 200px, so the role goes in the heading ONLY and the week bar keeps just the week number and the delta chip. (2) Its note read *"Fewer, heavier sets. Sharpen what you built."* — **grepped, zero hits in the codebase**, and it is peaking language under a block the mockup's own rail labels HYPERTROPHY. Filler; the note still comes from `weekNoteText`. (3) It renders pale lavender on white with most text barely legible. The app is dark-themed — an artifact of how the mockup was drawn, not a request, and no colour was taken from it.
  **One improvement ON the mockup:** it dropped the phase name from the sub-line, leaving `Hypertrophy` alive only as a 10px label under the strip. That buries the thing she asked to be able to see, so the sub-line names it in full, and the gate now fails if it falls out again.
  The strip's ticks and their block label are nested in one per-block column, so each name sits under its own four weeks **by construction** — the version before #13 spread four labels across the row with `justify-between` and hoped. Four mutations each turn `test:block-phases` §5 red: heading back to the block name; phase dropped from the sub-line; deload ticks in `--primary`; `B1`-style labels instead of `shortPhaseLabel`. §§1-4 are behavioural and untouched, which is exactly why splitting them out was worth it — the vocabulary and its agreement with the prose survived a full rewrite of the markup without a single edit.

- [x] **"4 WEEKS ALL SHOW HYPERTROPHY RATHER THAN GOING THROUGH EACH PHASE" — THE PLAN WAS RIGHT, THE SCREEN WAS WRONG.** Ashley, from the Full Program screen, then: *"Why are 4 weeks anatomical adaptation, followed by four weeks of hypertrophy. Shouldn't it change week by week through the phases."* The honest answer is no: a phase is a stimulus the body needs weeks of repeated exposure to before it adapts, so a phase per week means never staying anywhere long enough to get one. VISION.md commits to it — *"Mesocycle periodisation: four-week blocks with distinct phases"* — and `getPhaseSequence` (`periodization.ts:150`) builds four blocks of four. **But her instinct was pointing at something real.** The four weeks inside a block genuinely differ (her own block 1: 72.5 → 77.5 → 82.5kg, then a deload to 57.5, sets 11 → 11 → 11 → 8) and the biggest text on the page repeated one word four times, so it read as static. She then wrote the design brief herself: block name at top as *"Block 1: Anatomical Adaptation"*, and a horizontal week-by-week indicator naming Baseline / Building / Peak / Deload.
  **Built to that brief, display only — no migration, no deploy.** Everything it needs was already stored and already round-trips (`block_number`, `week_in_block`, `is_deload`, `is_calibration_week` in `mesocycle-shape.ts`). The 16-tick strip is gone; in the same two rows of vertical space there is now a **block rail** carrying the four block NAMES (it used to be four letters — B1 B2 B3 B4 — which could say where you were and never what any of it was for) and a **week strip** naming each week's job. The deload segment is amber in every state, matching the delta chip that already spends `--role-warn` on exactly that distinction: three weeks that mean *more* and one that means *less* must not look identical.
  **IT INVENTS NO VOCABULARY, AND THAT IS GATED.** Every label in `week-role.ts` is the word `buildProgressionNote` already writes into the paragraph below it. `test:block-phases` §3 compares the strip's name against the real generated note for all 80 weeks of five plans — a strip calling week 1 "Baseline" over a note saying "loads start deliberately light" would be one screen arguing with itself. That check earned its place immediately: the mutation that deletes the calibration branch was caught by §2 **and** independently by §3.
  Four mutations each turn it red: week 4 stops being the deload; the calibration branch is deleted; the component hardcodes the four names instead of calling `weekRole`; the deload renders in `--primary`. `shortPhaseLabel` reverse-maps through `PHASE_CONFIGS` rather than adding a stored column, because `phase_label` is persisted as a finished display string and the enum is gone by render time.

- [x] ~~NOTICED WHILE DOING THAT, NOT INVESTIGATED~~ **Measured and ruled on 2 Sep 2026 — see the rep-ranges entry at the end (Ashley: lift every Hypertrophy main lift to at least 6).** Original note: weeks 5-6 showed `Deadlifts 3×3-5` under a "Hypertrophy" heading whose own description promises *"moderate loads, higher volume, controlled tempo"*. 3-5 reps is a strength range. It may well be correct — a main lift often runs lower reps than the block's accessories, and `rep_shift` is a per-phase offset applied to a base range — but the heading and the prescription read against each other on the same screen, which is the shape of every honesty defect this repo has fixed. Worth measuring the actual rep ranges each phase produces for a main lift before deciding.

- [ ] **`swapped_in` IS STILL COUNTED AS EATEN, AND NOTHING WRITES IT ANY MORE — ASHLEY'S CALL.** `getTodayLedger`'s `eatenEvents` filter includes `swapped_in`, but `swapPoolMeal` stopped emitting those events on 12 Aug 2026 because *"swapping which option is assigned to a slot is a plan-state change, not a consumption event"*. So the only `swapped_in` rows that can exist are legacy ones written before that date, and each of them silently adds a meal's calories to that day's ring with no row on screen to explain it. **Not fixed unilaterally**: dropping it from the filter changes what "eaten" means and retroactively changes the numbers on every historical day that has one — CLAUDE.md puts "anything that changes what a metric measures" on Ashley. Two options when she wants it: stop counting them (past days' rings get lower and more honest), or leave them and accept that pre-12-Aug days can read high. No live user is affected today unless they scroll back.

- [ ] **A DEVIATION I OWE, UNPROMPTED.** CLAUDE.md: *"Dietary enforcement, injury filtering, and load prescription always get a plan before a build, even when the fix looks obvious."* All three load fixes above were **built before the plan existed** — diagnosis, fix and measurement in one pass, `docs/plans/one-weight-per-lift.md` written afterwards. Both changes only ever move a weight DOWN or leave it, and neither invents a behaviour, but the rule is plan-first and I did not follow it. Nothing is on `main`; the merge is still Ashley's.

- [x] **A FULL SWEEP OF ALL 116 GATES, AND A CORRECTION TO MY OWN REPORTING.** I told Ashley "nothing has failed so far" while the sweep was still buffering its output; it finished with nine not green. Three are this sandbox failing to reach Supabase (`meal-quality`, `schema-parity`, `week-load-consistency`) — not defects, and I had previously listed two of them as red gates without that qualifier. Six were real. Three are now fixed.
- [x] **THE SIGN-IN LINK'S GATE WAS GREPPING THE FILE THE LINK LEFT.** `test:auth-and-rls` looked in App.tsx for *"Already have an account? Sign in"*. The offer MOVED into `ConversationalOnboarding`'s composer when the fixed-position overlay was found sitting on top of the name box — the first control of the first screen, untappable. So a working feature had a red gate: the exact failure mode a source-text check has to avoid. It now asserts the copy where it lives AND the two halves of the wiring that connect it back to App.tsx, so deleting the words, unwiring the button, or dropping the prop each turn it red.
- [x] **THE RLS CHECK NAMED THE PRODUCTION PROJECT THREE TIMES OVER.** `test:deploy-path` §5 holds a property worth keeping — the live project is nameable in exactly one file, and every other mention of a ref is a script refusing to leave TEST. Reworking `verify-rls-live.ts` to say WHICH database it just cleared broke that by hardcoding both refs, twice each. `db-target.mjs` already exported `PROD_REF`, `TEST_REF` and `nameOf`; the script imports them now. The gate's own comment explains why refusal guards keep private copies — a guard that imports its definition of "safe" can be switched off everywhere by one edit — but that reasoning is about guards, and these were labels.
- [x] **INJURY COVERAGE: STALE EXPECTATIONS, NOT A WIDER FILTER.** Every injury's contraindicated count rose (knees 25→35, shoulders 47→62, elbows 10→18) because §6.2 added ~40 exercises, and the wipe table moved in three distinguishable ways. BETTER: wrists and ankles no longer lose ALL cardio below full_gym, and elbows/bodyweight no longer loses vertical_pull. NEWLY VISIBLE: shoulders/bodyweight and elbows/bodyweight now report wipes on patterns that had ZERO exercises at that tier before — the loop skipped them entirely, so they could not be reported. Every one of the new entries is a Backpack/Pike/Chair variant that genuinely loads the injured joint, so the trainee is no worse off than when the pattern was empty; the content gap just stopped being invisible. Table and counts recomputed FROM the database rather than hand-edited, after a hand-edit produced a second wrong answer.
- [ ] **THREE EXERCISES I ADDED ARE CATEGORISED AS CHEST WORK.** `Bodyweight Hip Hinge to Wall` (hamstrings, glutes), `Scapular Pull-Ups` (lats, rhomboids) and `Single-Arm Band Pulldown` (lats) all resolve to `isolation_chest`. Their `movement_pattern` values are CORRECT (`hip_hinge`, `vertical_pull`) — the gap is in `categorize`, whose isolation switch has no case for either, so they hit `default: return 'isolation_chest'`. That default's own comment already records this trapdoor firing twice before. **Measured impact today: none** — all three are bodyweight or band and prescribe `null` load, so no user sees a wrong weight. It still feeds coherence and substitution, and it will mis-price the first loaded exercise added with those patterns. `hip_hinge` has an obvious home (`isolation_hamstring`); `vertical_pull` has none — there is no lat-isolation category — and inventing one is a load-anchor decision, so this waits for Ashley per the load-prescription rule. `test:categorize-precedence` also covers only 158 of 185 exercises; extending the snapshot would freeze these three wrong values, so it waits on the same call.

- [x] **THE COACH COULD FILE A SCHEDULE PROBLEM AS A MEMORY NOTE INSTEAD OF FIXING IT.** `record_fact`'s kind list includes `hard_constraint`, so *"I can't train on Tuesdays"* matches it on the words alone — and two tools competed for one sentence with no rule saying which wins. Filing it only writes it down: the plan carries on prescribing Tuesday sessions the user then misses, so the app looks like it helped and changed nothing. The memory rules now resolve it explicitly in favour of `propose_schedule_change` (or `propose_rest_day` for a single day), while leaving `record_fact` for availability no schedule can express — *"my shifts change every week"*.
- [x] **MORE REPS, LESS WEIGHT — BUILT, AND NARROWER THAN THE RULING (`docs/plans/reps-up-weight-down.md`).** A real plan showed Lateral Raises at *12-17 @ 6kg* then *13-18 @ 4kg*: more work for a third less weight, which reads as a demotion. The standards estimate is recomputed each week against that week's rep target and falls as the reps climb; the ramp's `Math.min` was a ceiling with no floor, so the displayed weight followed it down. Ashley chose "the weight never drops". **What shipped holds the weight only when the REP TARGET WENT UP** — building the sentence literally caused a measured regression, and the narrowing covers every case the rule was chosen for. `test:frozen-weeks` §1: **11 offenders → 0**, and its threshold moved from a parked baseline of 10 to a hard 0. Audit: **0 failures / 13,967**. Carry loads bit-identical to baseline, verified by diff.
- [x] **THREE OF MY ASSUMPTIONS IN THAT BUILD WERE WRONG, AND THE MEASUREMENTS CAUGHT ALL THREE.** (1) I put the floor inside the unverified RAMP and the output did not move — Lateral Raises never use the ramp, because a 2kg dumbbell notch is 33% of a 6kg lift so `loadStepUnaffordable` makes it reps-progressed. (2) **I read the audit as 54 failures → 7 and called it an improvement. The real baseline was 0** — I had compared against the committed `audit-report.txt`, which predates other fixes, instead of re-running with my change stashed. My change had introduced 7, not removed 47. (3) The ramp-side floor from attempt (1) was still live and carries DO ramp, so it was silently flooring them — 6 `block_transition_jump` failures. Removing it returned every carry to baseline. A fourth: the floored number leaked into the ramp anchors and was handed to whatever variation rotated in next (4 `rotation_relative_load` failures), so the anchors now take the pre-floor number and only the display takes the held one.
- [x] **FIFTEEN EXERCISES WERE PRICED AS CHEST ISOLATION — FIXED, ALL THREE GROUPS.** `categorize`'s isolation switch had no case for five movement patterns, so they hit `default: return 'isolation_chest'` — bench press × 0.38. Ashley chose to fix all three groups properly rather than patch the two dangerous ones. Measured, male 80kg full gym, novice/intermediate/advanced:

  | | before | after |
  |---|---|---|
  | Face Pulls | 27.5kg | 10 / 15 / 20kg |
  | Reverse Pec Deck | 27.5kg | 10 / 15 / 20kg |
  | Rear Delt Flyes | 14kg per hand | 6 / 8 / 10kg per hand |
  | Straight-Arm Pulldown | 27.5kg | 15 / 20 / 25kg |
  | Russian Twist | 27.5kg | **no weight** |
  | Cable Woodchops | 27.5kg | **no weight** |

  **Rear delts** get `isolation_rear_delt`, deliberately the SAME fraction as the side delt (0.19 of bench): the two heads are comparable, and the existing per-side halving separates the implements by itself — a dumbbell fly lands per hand on the lateral-raise number, a cable or machine version at roughly double because it is both arms. **Lat isolation** gets `isolation_lat` anchored to the ROW family, not bench — that is the entire point, since this work is limited by pulling strength. **Core gets no anchor at all**, on purpose: trunk rotation is not predicted by any barbell lift, so a fraction would be the same guess in a new place. All seven core movements now behave alike — progress by reps and tempo, as Plank and Dead Bug already did — which also retires the 27.5kg loaded spinal rotation. Two more fell out of the same fix: a bodyweight hip hinge is hamstring work, and a wall sit is quad work.
- [x] **AND THE CHECK THAT FOUND THEM ALMOST DIDN'T EXIST.** `test:categorize-precedence` was red only because its snapshot was stale (158 of 185 exercises). Regenerating it from the database would have turned it green while blessing every one of these fifteen — a gate passing because it was told the wrong answer is the right one. So the snapshot was regenerated AND an independent check added: an exercise in `isolation_chest` whose own `primary_muscles` contain no chest did not get chosen, it fell through. That check is derived from the data, not from a list I wrote, which is why it found twelve I did not know about.

- [ ] **7,340 LOAD CLAMPS THE CODE ITSELF SAYS NOT TO TRUST.** Surfaced by the quality sweep's log while verifying the load floor, then measured on the audit: **7,340 clamp warnings, identical with and without that change**, so entirely pre-existing. The warning's own words: *"This is a safety net, not a fix: something upstream produced a wrong number and should be traced, not just the clamp trusted."* 24 distinct exercises hit a ceiling; the top one is **Single-Leg Dumbbell Calf Raise at 2,829 of them**, computing 50, 52, 54, 58, 60, 64, 66, 72kg against a 48kg implement ceiling — so it is not a rounding edge, it is a number that climbs well past the cap and gets silently pulled back. Others: Dumbbell Floor Press (556), Dumbbell Shrugs (553), Shrugs (478), Farmer's Walk (379). The audit passes at 0 failures BECAUSE the clamp catches these, which is exactly the shape of a check satisfied by the wrong thing — the plan is safe, and the calculation underneath is wrong. Worth tracing the calf raise first: a per-side isolation lift being priced above a full dumbbell rack is a big, specific error and likely one shared root.
  UPDATE 1 Sep 2026 — **traced, and my "likely one shared root" guess was wrong.** Measured every exercise that reaches its implement ceiling anywhere in a 240-cell grid (4 experience × 5 bodies × 4 rep ranges × 3 RPEs). `Single-Leg Dumbbell Calf Raise` hits it in **56 of 240** cells; the next worst is 23, and **every other one clamps only in the top corner** — a 120kg advanced male — which is the honest "ran out of dumbbell" case the ceiling was written for. So it is one mis-modelled exercise plus a long tail of the safety net working, not a systemic fault. The cause: `isolation_calf` is 0.65 × squat, calibrated for a MACHINE where the machine supplies the load, applied to a lift where the trainee is standing on one foot and the leg already carries full bodyweight before the dumbbell exists. An intermediate 80kg man is told to hold **36kg in one hand** balancing on a step; advanced wants 60kg and gets clamped to 48. **Investigated only — nothing built.** Load prescription and the numbers are Ashley's: `docs/plans/the-48kg-calf-raise.md` has the current table, three options, a recommendation, and a candidate table to rule on the way she ruled on kettlebell swings. NOTE ON DENOMINATORS: "7,340" counts warnings logged in one audit run (the same prescription once per week per profile); the 56/240 above counts distinct grid cells. Not comparable — the ranking is the part that carries. **CONFIRMED ON A THIRD CORPUS:** the full plan-quality sweep (9,216 profiles × 16 weeks) logs **88,046 clamp warnings, 100% of them this one exercise** — no other catalogue entry reaches its ceiling anywhere in it, because that grid's body weights are ordinary rather than reaching 120kg, so the honest top-corner clamps never fire and only the structural one is left. That sweep scores **11.45 / 12 with 0 plans below the 7.2 floor** throughout: a quality score measures structure, progression, time fit and selection, not whether 48kg in one hand on a step is an instruction anyone can follow.

- [ ] **A DELOAD CAN COME IN HEAVIER THAN THE WEEK BEFORE IT.** Two cases, found by a check added during the above and present identically with and without it, so pre-existing: `full_gym/full_body/intermediate Seated Cable Row wk12: 40 → 45` and `minimalist/full_body/intermediate Dumbbell Floor Press wk16: 18 → 20`. A deload is supposed to be the easy week. Pinned in the gate BY NAME rather than by a count, so fixing one while breaking another still fails — a budget of "at most 2" is the exact shape §1 spent eleven offenders learning to distrust.
  UPDATE 1 Sep 2026: the full_gym case stopped reproducing when the machine-floor batch added 15 full_gym candidates and changed which exercises that seeded plan picks. **Displaced, not fixed** — the mechanism is untouched and the minimalist case still reproduces, so this stays open. The gate's ledger now lists only the minimalist case; if the full_gym one re-manifests under a future pool it fails by name as a NEW rise.
  UPDATE 1 Sep 2026 (later): **the minimalist case is now genuinely fixed, and by something else.** `enforceOneWeightPerPrescription` (see the entry above) settles one weight per lift per week; Dumbbell Floor Press had been holding two in week 15, and this check compares the deload against the LOWER of them (`Math.min`). One weight per week removes the disagreement the comparison was reading. The gate's pinned list is now **empty** — which makes it stronger, not weaker: any deload rise at all now fails by name.
- [x] **AND A CORRECTION ON HOW I FOUND IT.** My first probe used `RPE 8`, got 6kg for both rep ranges, and concluded the rep target didn't explain the drop — I nearly went looking in the wrong place. The real week uses `RPE 7-8`, where the estimate does fall 6 → 4. I had guessed an input instead of reading it off the generated week: the same error shape as the calorie misdiagnosis, arithmetic on assumed inputs rather than actual ones. Reading the real week's `intensity` off the mesocycle took one line.

- [x] **"REST DAY TODAY" WAS ANSWERED WITH A SENTENCE AND NO WRITE.** Caught live from Ashley's phone, 31 Aug 2026. She said *"Rest day today"*; the coach replied *"I will mark today as a rest day for you"* and could not have — no tool records a rest. `classifyDay` ends `dateStr < todayStr ? 'missed' : 'due'`, so the day she declared would have shown **missed** the next morning: the app telling her off for a decision she made deliberately and announced at the time. The exact defect `swapped_for_activity` was added to fix, caught again in its plainer form — that column needs an ACTIVITY, and resting is the answer with no activity in it. **The prompt already forbade the reply in as many words** (*"Do not say a day has been marked… unless you actually called a tool that does it"*). The rule was there and had nothing to call; a rule with no tool behind it is one the model routes around, so this adds the tool rather than restating the rule louder. Ashley's ruling: record it, **but confirm first** — so `propose_rest_day` rides the propose→confirm→execute→receipt rail, and a new `deliberate_rest` column gets a state of its own (`rest_chosen`, "rest day you chose") rather than collapsing into `rest`, which would rewrite the plan's history into one where Monday was never a training day.
- [x] **AND THE SECOND LIE IN THE SAME CONVERSATION: "Got tomorrow morning locked in."** Nothing in the app stores an intended training time — no field, no screen, no row — so that sentence was true of nowhere. Unlike the rest day, there is no tool to add here, because there is nothing it would write to; the fix is the model not saying it. The prompt now bans "locked in", "booked in", "got that scheduled" and the rest of the family by name, and says what to do instead: acknowledge it as something they told you, not something you wrote down.
- [x] **A TEST OF MINE FAILED FOR THE RIGHT REASON AND TAUGHT ME THE DOMAIN.** I asserted a chosen rest day was distinct from "a day the plan never prescribed" and used Sunday — which IS in the fixture plan, as a zero-exercise day, and so classifies as `recovery`, not `rest`. Three states that all look like a quiet day and mean three different things: nothing was ever asked of you (`rest`), something gentle was (`recovery`), something real was and you chose not to (`rest_chosen`). The gate now pins all three apart.

- [x] **"SEE FULL PROGRAM" LOOKED LIKE A DIFFERENT APP.** Ashley, from her phone: *"in exercise under see full program the app looks completely different to the exercise section. they should look the same as the main exercise section."* It was a `<Table>` — Exercise / Sets / Reps / Rest columns, a superset badge in a spacer cell, three always-visible icon buttons per row — while today's screen is hairline-separated lines with a tier label and a mono `3×6-8 · 42.5kg` summary.
- [x] **THE SAME DEFECT, THE THIRD TIME, AND THE FIRST TWO FIXES EXPLAIN WHY.** PeekPanel had exactly this problem and was fixed by sharing `ExerciseLine`; its header comment promised the two surfaces "cannot drift apart again", and for the ROW that held. **The row was the small half.** What makes two screens look like different apps is everything around it — the section labels, the superset rail and its "alternate — no rest between" instruction, and whether a day is a list at all. Nothing shared the DAY, so the program view was never touched by either fix. `ReadOnlyDayList` now owns the whole day; PeekPanel is a header and a call to it, ExercisePlan lost 104 lines, and `test:one-day-one-look` fails if any browse surface grows its own day-rendering, if either renders a table again, or if a FOURTH file renders an exercise line directly.
- [x] **AND THE NEW GATE'S FIRST RUN FAILED ON ITS OWN PROSE.** Two checks went red against a correct implementation because they matched the comments explaining the fix — *"this was a `<Table>` —"* and *"it never imports SetGrid"*. A check a comment can satisfy, or break, is the same defect this file exists to catch wearing the reviewer's clothes. It strips comments first now, the way the other source-reading gates already did.
- [x] **A THIRD CHECK WAS READING THE FILE THE MENU LEFT.** `test:exercise-detail` asserted the technique panel opens from the peek's menu — but the menu moved into the shared component, so a working feature went red. Fixed the way the sign-in gate was earlier the same day: assert the menu where it lives AND the prop threading that reaches it, never one half alone. It also now pins that the program view deliberately offers history and NOT technique, so that absence reads as a decision rather than an omission.

- [x] **THE WELCOME MESSAGE NOW SAYS THE PLAN HAS A SHAPE.** Ashley, after the phase brief landed: does onboarding give a brief description of what the plan will be like? It did not. The opener named day one and stopped, so someone who read it and closed the app never learned the plan had a shape at all — `buildCoachPhaseBrief` covers this the moment they SPEAK, but nothing covered the moment before that. One sentence appended to the same message (still one message, per her earlier cut from four): *"16 weeks, 4 blocks. Week one's light while we find your weights — I'll say when it steps up."* Every number is read off the generated mesocycle by `planShapeFromMesocycle`; an empty plan produces no sentence rather than a confident "16 weeks", and blocks and "getting harder" are only claimed when there is more than one block to claim it about. A calibration week one is always named as deliberate — the person most likely to quit over an easy first week is the one who was never told it was on purpose. Measured at 412px with `render:screens`: 9 lines, chips still above the fold.
- [x] **A GATE THAT PASSED TWO MUTATIONS BEFORE IT PASSED FOR THE RIGHT REASON.** The first version searched all of `ChatAssistant.tsx` for `totalWeeks: mesocycle.length` and stayed GREEN while the derivation was mutated to a hardcoded 16 — because the phase-brief wiring, 400 lines away, contains that exact string. The second scoped the search to the function and still passed a mutation that assigned `blocks = 4` and left the real expression sitting unused beside it. Both are the same defect this repo keeps hitting: **a check satisfied by something other than the thing it is about.** The fix was not a tighter regex — it was moving the derivation out of the component into a pure exported function and running it against real plans, where a wrong number is simply a wrong number. Ten mutations now turn it red, including both that got through.

- [x] **THE COACH DIDN'T KNOW WHICH PHASE YOU WERE IN — 31 Aug 2026, and half of Ashley's report was generous.** She said *"it knows which phase of training the user is in ie, adaptation, hypertrophy etc but never says anything about it"*. **It did not know.** The prompt carried a full textbook — what Anatomical Adaptation, Hypertrophy Accumulation and Intensification each mean — and the client sent only the week's exercises, the coach note and pending suggestions. `phase_label`, `phase_focus`, `block_number`, `week_in_block`, `is_deload` and `isCalibrationWeek` all existed on every week of the plan and **not one of them ever reached the chat**. So it could explain periodization in the abstract and could not place her inside it, which is exactly why it only sounded knowledgeable when asked a direct question.

- [x] **ASHLEY'S RULING ON HOW FORWARD TO BE: "when a plan is built give a quick high level of the weeks to come then when something changes."** `buildCoachPhaseBrief` now reports position (week N of M, block, phase, what the phase is for) plus one of three instructions: **SPEAK UP** with a short high-level shape when the plan is new and unwalked-through; **SPEAK UP** briefly when the week has turned over since they last spoke; otherwise **do not volunteer it**. That last branch is the one that stops it nagging — narrating the phase daily teaches people to skim the opening paragraph, which then hides the days it mattered. Deload and calibration weeks are always named, because both read as going backwards to someone who isn't told.

- [x] **BOTH MOMENTS ARE COMPUTED FROM THE PLAN ITSELF, not a stored flag.** "New plan" needs week 1 AND a plan under three days old — week 1 alone is not enough, since someone who trains rarely can sit in it for a fortnight. "Week turned over" compares the last message's timestamp against the week's own start date, derived from the plan's creation date, so it stays correct after a rebuild and cannot drift out of sync. Gated by `test:coach-phase-brief`; four mutations bite, including the two that matter most — never speaking up, and speaking up every single turn.

- [ ] **NEEDS A DEPLOY: `npm run deploy:functions:prod -- chat-gemini`.** The client half ships with the frontend; the prompt half does not. Until that runs the coach still receives nothing about where the trainee is.

- [x] **"WANT ME TO REMEMBER *THAT*?" — the app offered to remember the word "that", 31 Aug 2026.** Ashley said *"I cant train on tuesday"*; the coach recorded it as a memory fact, and `buildIntentProposal` ended every branch in `|| 'that'`. A scheduling sentence has no food or exercise for the model to put in `target_phrase`, so the fallback fired and a **placeholder was rendered on a confirm button as if it were the content**. The fallback is her own words now — `origin_verbatim_quote`, already computed two lines above for the scope key — across all five append kinds, and the literal `'that'` is gone from the file so it cannot come back on a path this test doesn't walk.

- [x] **TAPPING PAUSE DELETED THE TIMER SCREEN.** `pauseRound` sets `running: false`, and `ToolsTab`'s `roundHoldsScreen` only held the screen while `running` — so pausing mid-workout dropped her back to the setup form, which is what "it exits the timer" meant. You pause to catch your breath, not to lose your place. A paused round holds the screen now.

- [x] **AND THE DOCK CHIP REPEATED THE SCREEN IT SAT ON.** "Round 1/8 · Work · 0:29" in 12px, over a display already shouting the same thing in colour edge to edge — and positioned across the Pause button. It's suppressed while the round field is showing, decided by the real route rather than a guess, and still appears the moment you navigate away, which is its actual job.

- [ ] **STILL WRONG, AND IT NEEDS A DEPLOY: the coach reached for the wrong tool.** "I can't train on Tuesday", said twice, should reach `propose_schedule_change` — §3e of the prompt says exactly that, and the tool went live this morning. It called `record_fact` instead. The prompt has no precedence rule saying a day-availability statement is a schedule request before it is a fact to remember. Fixing it is a prompt edit plus another `deploy:functions:prod -- chat-gemini`, so it is Ashley's call whether to do it now or batch it.

- [x] **EVERYTHING IS LIVE, 31 Aug 2026 — and the RLS check finally ran against the real database.** Ashley merged to `main` (`1da0817`), deployed `chat-gemini` to production, and ran `verify:rls -- --prod`: **28 tables, 28 returned nothing, 0 refused, 0 handed over rows, 0 never answered.** The "0 never answered" is the part that makes it mean something — the run genuinely reached production rather than failing into a green tick, which is exactly what the INCONCLUSIVE work earlier that day was for. Her data lives on this project (the deployed app points at it), so "an anonymous caller sees nothing" is a real result and not the vacuous empty-database case the script warns about.

- [x] **AND THE VERY LAST LINE IT PRINTED WAS A BROKEN COMMAND — the fourth handed over in two days.** The service-key hint read `npm run verify:rls --service-key=THE_KEY`, with no `--` separator, so npm would swallow the flag as its own and the script would never see the key: following it would silently reprint the same note. The separator is now unconditional in that string. **Four for four, all the same shape**: bash syntax to a PowerShell user, two placeholders meant to be replaced and pasted literally, and now a missing separator. Every one printed by me, none caught by a gate, all found by Ashley running them.

- [x] **THE ROUND TIMER WAS A CARD, NOT A FIELD — 31 Aug 2026, spotted by Ashley comparing the design against her phone.** The whole point of the colour states is that the colour IS the state, readable across a gym without picking the phone up. What shipped filled about 60% of the screen inside the page's padding, with dark showing above and below. **`RoundField` was `position: absolute`, and ToolsTab wrapped it in `className="relative"` with `minHeight: 60vh`** — absolute positioning resolves against the nearest POSITIONED ancestor, so the full-bleed field became a card in a 60vh box. Now `fixed`, at `z-30` under the tab bar's `z-40`, and ToolsTab's wrapper is unpositioned. Measured in a real browser at 390×844: flush to all four edges, exactly `viewport − 64px`, tab bar uncovered.

- [x] **MY OWN PREVIEW HARNESS IS WHY IT SHIPPED LOOKING RIGHT.** `render-timer-field.tsx` drew each state inside `position:relative;width:390px;height:700px` — precisely the containing block the real app never provides. So `absolute` filled the harness perfectly and the design review passed on a picture that could not have shown the bug. **Second time this week the same shape has bitten**: the onboarding gate photographed a hand-copied replica of the composer and could not see a layer that only existed in App.tsx. The harness now renders each panel in an **iframe**, which is genuinely its own viewport, so `fixed` behaves as it does on a phone and the picture cannot flatter the code.

- [x] **NEW GATE `test:timer-field-fills-screen`, and the first version of it was too loose.** It asked whether the field covered >85% of the viewport height; the absolute-positioning mutation passed that by stretching to the **document** height — 2056px on an 844px screen — which is just as wrong as too small and reads as "fills the screen" to a threshold. It now asserts the height is **exactly** viewport minus tab-bar. It also asserts the other half of the bug directly, since the geometry test renders the field without ToolsTab and cannot otherwise see it: **ToolsTab's wrapper must not be positioned and must not force a height.** Both mutations — field back to `absolute`, wrapper back to `relative` — each turn it red.

- [x] **PRODUCTION HAS THE SECURITY MIGRATIONS — VERIFIED, NOT ASSUMED, 31 Aug 2026.** Read directly off `sdkhuczcfnqqimdgfiks`'s applied-migration list: `20260830120000_scope_every_table_to_its_owner` and `20260830130000_close_the_usage_counter_to_anon` are both there. **`npm run db:push-both` ran.** Two things follow and both retire warnings I had been repeating: the live database is scoped to its owner rather than wide open, and `owner_id` exists — so finishing onboarding will NOT fail when the app stamps a profile, which I had told Ashley three times that it would. **What this does NOT establish** is that the policies behave correctly once applied; that is what `npm run verify:rls -- --prod` is for, and it has still never been pointed at production.

- [x] **LOGGED MEALS ARE COUNTED TWICE AND THE LIST CANNOT SHOW IT, 31 Aug 2026 — found by Ashley on her phone.** Her four planned meals total 1805 kcal; the ring read **2887**. The difference is exactly 1082 = lunch (553) + dinner (529), each counted a second time. `getTodayLedger` sums EVERY `confirmed`/`swapped_in`/`extra` event, but `MealPlan.tsx`'s `loggedBySlot` keeps only the LAST event per slot (`next[e.slot] = e`) — so duplicates are invisible in the meal list, each row shows a single ✓, and the per-row Unlog voids one `client_id` while the others keep counting. **How the duplicates are created is not yet established** — that needs her actual rows, which this sandbox cannot reach. Workaround until fixed: tap Logged repeatedly on the affected meal, once per hidden duplicate.
  UPDATE 1 Sep 2026 — **the harm is fixed and the write-side window is closed; the ledger's ability to HOLD two events is not, and must not be.** `meal_events` is append-only by design: the events are the audit trail, so collapsing duplicates at read time would be inventing history. What was wrong was what the screen did with it. `loggedEventsBySlot` now owns the grouping and keeps every event, the row prints the **summed** kcal so it can only ever agree with the totals ring above it, a duplicated slot says so in words ("Logged 2 times, so today's totals are counting 840 kcal for it instead of 420"), and undo voids **every** copy via `voidMealEvents` — tapping it no longer flips the button to "not logged" while calories keep counting. The workaround below (tap Logged repeatedly) is obsolete.
  **AND THE CAUSE TURNED OUT TO BE READABLE AFTER ALL** — I had said it needed her live rows. It did not. `logMealEaten` is the only writer in the app, and the row's busy flag cleared as soon as the event was QUEUED, while the ledger refresh that would flip the button to "Logged" was still in flight. For one round-trip the button was live again and still reading "Log this meal": a second tap wrote a second event. `reloadLogged` returns its promise now and the handler awaits it. What I could not read from here is whether that window is what produced HER duplicates specifically — it is the only path that exists, but the rows themselves are on production and this sandbox cannot reach it.
  **WHAT THE FIX DOES NOT REACH: yesterday.** The Meals tab only ever renders TODAY (`getSessionDateContext(profile.id).date`), so a duplicate on a past date cannot be cleared from anywhere in the app — and `dashboard-data.ts` reads the same `ledger.eaten` sum, so that day keeps its inflated calories and can still be counting toward the 14-day protein streak on a total the user never ate. Correct going forward, uncorrectable backwards. Fixing it means either a date-picker on the Meals tab or a one-off cleanup, both bigger than this and neither obviously wanted — flagged, not built.
  **THE ALTERNATIVE HYPOTHESIS, RULED OUT RATHER THAN IGNORED.** `getTodayLedger` counts `swapped_in` toward `eaten`, and a `swapped_in` row shows in NO meal row — so a pair of legacy swap events would produce exactly her arithmetic (lunch + dinner, one extra time each) with nothing on screen to explain it. It is not that: `swapPoolMeal` stopped writing those events on **12 Aug 2026** (`782139b`, "swapping which option is assigned to a slot is a plan-state change, not a consumption event"), nineteen days before she saw this, and the ledger is date-scoped — no `swapped_in` row can exist for 31 Aug. Duplicate `confirmed` events are the only remaining explanation, and the double-tap window above is the only path that writes them.
  Gated: `test:meal-roundtrip` §14 (the ledger holds two, the screen is given two, the shown kcal equals the counted kcal, undo clears all of them, and a `swapped_in` is not shown as a log the user made) and §15 (the component is actually wired to the shared helper — a correct store function nothing calls is this repo's recurring failure mode). Three mutations each turn §14 red: last-event-wins grouping, voiding only the first copy, and counting a swap as a log.

- [ ] **AND A CORRECTION I OWE THE RECORD: I DIAGNOSED THE SECOND NUMBER WRONG, CONFIDENTLY, TWICE.** The same screen showed a ring target of 1801 and a "How your targets are set" card reading TARGET **1983**. Working backwards from 1801 with textbook BMR arithmetic in my head, I concluded the app was holding a stale ~70 kg weight and told Ashley she was being under-fed by ~180 kcal every day. **Running the app's own functions: no weight between 60 and 90 kg produces 1801.** 1801 is her real DYNAMIC_CSCS per-day target — exact match on all four values, kcal and all three macros — and 1983 is the STATIC baseline the derivation card deliberately always explains. Both correct. **The macro split had been saying so the whole time**: a different weight scales every macro together, and her carbs differed by 67 g while fat differed by 2 g — the signature of a different calculation, not a different body. I read past the shape of the numbers because the story fitted. `scripts/probe-target-mismatch.ts` keeps the reproduction.

- [ ] **THE ONE REAL FAULT ON THAT CARD IS SMALLER AND IS ASHLEY'S CALL:** it labels the static baseline **TARGET**, in the highlight colour, directly under a ring showing a different number, with nothing saying which is which. It was persuasive enough to send me down the wrong path for two messages, which is decent evidence it misleads. Options: name both (*"Baseline 1983 · today 1801"*), or drop the baseline from the card entirely. Recommend the first — the derivation is genuinely useful, it just should not call itself the target.

- [x] **`npm run verify:rls -- --prod` NOW ASKS FOR THE KEY, because the command line was the defect.** The instruction to run this check failed three times on Ashley's machine and not once was it her fault: first as bash syntax PowerShell cannot parse, then `PASTE_KEY_HERE` pasted literally, then `<the real key>` pasted literally and hitting PowerShell's reserved `<`. Now: one short command, no long URL, no placeholder, and it prompts for the key when it needs it. Pasted angle brackets, quotes and trailing whitespace are stripped rather than sent to the database.

- [x] **AND BUILDING THAT PROMPT EXPOSED A WORSE ONE: `--prod` WOULD HAVE USED THE TEST KEY AGAINST PRODUCTION.** The prompt did not appear in testing, because `.env.local` supplies a key and the fallback happily took it. Production would have been handed a credential it has never heard of, replied *"Invalid API key"* — an error carrying a PostgREST code — and this script counted any coded error as *the database refused me*, which is a **pass**. So the command written to check production would have printed **PASSED for PRODUCTION** on the strength of a key production rejected outright. An explicit target now never borrows `.env.local`'s key, and a rejected key counts as an unanswered table rather than a refusal. **Third time this week a check has been satisfied by the wrong thing** — after the migration that DROPped derived policy names, and the gate satisfied by an undo branch.

- [x] **THE SECURITY CHECK SAID "PASSED" OVER 28 FAILED CONNECTIONS, 30 Aug 2026 — the worst defect found this week.** Every error counted as a pass. The code's own comment explained why — *"a policy that denies rather than filters shows up as an error"* — which is true of a real refusal and false of a connection that never arrived. Pointed at production with an unreachable host it printed **"PASSED for PRODUCTION"** having spoken to nothing. A wrong URL, a stray character in a pasted key, no internet, or the free tier auto-pausing after a quiet week would every one of them have read as *your data is safe*. It now separates an answer from a silence — PostgREST replies carry an error code, dropped connections do not — and a run with any unanswered table is **INCONCLUSIVE**, exits non-zero, and names the likely causes with the paused-project case first. Verified live: 28 unreachable tables now produce INCONCLUSIVE and exit 1. The pass path could not be exercised from here, as this sandbox cannot reach Supabase at all.

- [x] **AND THE COMMAND COULD NOT BE TYPED ON HER MACHINE.** The instruction was `RLS_TARGET_URL=... npm run verify:rls`, which is bash syntax; Ashley runs PowerShell, where it fails with *"is not recognized as a name of a cmdlet"*. The script's own printed hint had the same bug, so following it would have failed a second time. It takes `--url` / `--anon-key` / `--service-key` flags now, which parse identically in PowerShell, Command Prompt and a Mac terminal, and every hint it prints is the flag form. Second handoff instruction in two days that was wrong about the environment it was going to be run in.

- [x] **THE SECURITY CHECK PASSED ON THE WRONG DATABASE, 30 Aug 2026 — and said so in a way nobody would notice.** Ashley ran `npm run verify:rls` as instructed; it reported *"PASSED — no user table gave up a single row without an account"* across 28 tables. It had read **TEST**. `.env.local` points there by design, and the script printed only the bare project ref — a twenty-character string nobody is expected to recognise on sight. A security check whose reassuring line can describe the wrong database is worse than no check, because it stops the real one from being run. **My handoff instruction was the defect**: I listed the command as step 2 without noting where it aims. It now names the database in plain words (**PRODUCTION** / **TEST** / **an UNRECOGNISED project**), says outright that a non-production run covers none of your live data, and prints the exact command to aim it at production — via `RLS_TARGET_URL`/`RLS_TARGET_ANON_KEY`, so nobody has to hand-edit `.env.local`, which its own comment warns not to commit.

- [x] **THE SWAP LIST GOT DEEPER, AND THE HOLE THAT MATTERED MOST WAS NOT THE ONE THE AUDIT NAMED, 30 Aug 2026 — §6.2.** Measured first with the same function the ⇄ button calls. **A sore shoulder with no equipment left 35 exercises with nothing to swap to** — every press, every triceps movement, every raise — while the identical injury with a full gym stranded none. So it hit precisely the person training at home who had just hurt themselves. The cause: the fallback map pairs pushing with pushing, and an injury takes the *joint*, not the pattern, so both stages came back empty together. **Ashley's ruling**, choosing between offering gentler pushing and swapping the work out: *"swap in different work"* — the app never suggests loading a joint somebody has told us hurts, so the alternative to a press is legs, core or pulling, said plainly. Now 0 stranded, at every tier and every injury.

- [x] **27 NEW EXERCISES, AIMED RATHER THAN SPRINKLED.** Constrained to what each thin tier actually owns — bodyweight gets only bodyweight, a pull-up bar and a weighted backpack, so an entry that closes a bodyweight gap with a dumbbell closes nothing. Backpack entries are over-represented on purpose: a backpack is allowed at every tier, so one entry lands in all four pools. Like-for-like on the same 157 exercises: home_gym exercises with two-or-fewer alternatives **21 → 12**, minimalist **30 → 18**, bodyweight **45 → 40** and its five zero-option cases **→ 0**. Pull-Ups went from **1 alternative to 5** at home_gym and minimalist — it had fewer options with equipment than without, which was backwards.

- [x] **THE AUDIT'S OWN §6.2 NUMBERS DO NOT REPRODUCE.** It reported a bodyweight user getting "squats 2, bench 2, deadlift 4, overhead press 2, rows 2, pull-ups 1"; the real figures were 3, 3, 3, 3, 4, 4, and **three of the six exercise names it used are not in the catalogue at all**. The shortage was real but not where it pointed. Recorded because it is the second time this week an audit figure has been carried forward without being re-derived.

- [x] **A METRIC THAT LIED, AND A NOTE THAT LIED.** Overhead Press at bodyweight went from 3 alternatives to 2 — an improvement: those patterns used to be empty, so the fallback padded the list with wall sits and planks, and a raw count rewards that. The comparison now counts only same-kind alternatives. Separately, the first version of the new coaching note said *"this rests your sore shoulder"* whenever it fired — but it fires for somebody with no equipment and nothing sore, which is the app inventing an injury nobody reported. Both caught by reading the measured numbers rather than the intent.

- [ ] **A SECOND REGRESSION, FOUND BY THE QUALITY SCORER AFTER THE FACT — same area, same plan.** The full 9,216-profile sweep came back **11.47/12 against a baseline of 11.51**, and the whole 0.04 sits in Selection (**1.90 → 1.87**); every other dimension is unchanged and plans below the floor stay at 0. The scorer names it: *"Backpack Lateral Raise uses weighted backpack when this minimalist profile has a better-loading option for the same pattern and tier"*. The ranking is not broken — bands and backpacks are already rated lowest — but the preference is a weighting, not a rule, so enlarging a pattern's pool with low-ranked entries means one occasionally wins a slot from somebody who owns dumbbells. **There is no content-only fix**: equipment sets are per-tier, and the entries that close the bodyweight holes are the same ones that are second-best at minimalist. Making a low-ranked implement never beat an available better one is plan-generation work and belongs with the load defect below, in one plan rather than two.

- [ ] **A REGRESSION THIS ROUND CAUSES AND DOES NOT FIX — needs its own plan before anyone touches it.** `test:frozen-weeks` budgets a known defect (a rep increase paid for with a lighter weight) at 10; with the new entries it is **11**. Reproduced: a full-gym novice's Lateral Raises go `3x12-17 @ 6kg` in week 9 to `3x13-18 @ 4kg` in week 10 — more reps, less weight, inside one block. **No particular entry causes it**: removing the two backpack shoulder entries leaves 11, reverting the regression-list additions leaves 11, removing all 27 returns exactly 10, and adding any single one keeps it at 10. The diff touches no load-prescription code — more entries simply re-roll which profiles land in a state that already existed. **The budget was not raised**, because that is the same move as skipping a failing test. The underlying bug is load prescription and gets a plan first.

- [x] **THE SIGN-IN OFFER WAS SITTING ON TOP OF THE NAME BOX, 30 Aug 2026 — found by Ashley on her phone, live.** She could not type anything at all: the first control of the first screen, for every new user. Both the offer and the composer were `fixed` to the bottom of the screen at the same layer, and the one rendered later took every tap. **The comment directly above it claimed it was offered "BESIDE onboarding rather than in front of it"** — the code did the exact opposite of what its own justification said, and the reason given for that placement was to avoid touching the onboarding component and its gates. Keeping a component untouched is not worth covering its only input. It now lives inside the composer's own column, so the browser lays the two out and overlap is impossible rather than merely unlikely. It shows before the first thing you say and steps aside after, since "new or returning?" is a question about the first ten seconds.

- [x] **NOTHING IN NINETY-ODD GATES COULD HAVE SEEN IT, and that is the real finding.** Every gate reasons about DATA — loads, floors, policies, tool wiring — and `render-screens` photographs a hand-copied **replica** of the composer, so it cannot see a second layer that only exists in App.tsx. There was no layout engine anywhere in the suite. New gate `test:onboarding-reachable` runs the **real production build in a real browser at 390×844**, fakes Supabase at the network layer so the app under test is byte-for-byte the one that deploys, and asks the only question that matters: tap the middle of this control — what do you actually hit? Putting the old overlay back turns it red and names the culprit by class. It skips loudly rather than failing where there is no browser.

- [x] **THE FIRST VERSION OF THAT GATE CRIED WOLF ON CORRECT CODE.** It reported the send button as covered; the send button is dim until there is something to send, so taps passing through it is deliberate. Disabled controls are now exempt — and the exemption is closed from the other side by typing and re-checking, so "it was disabled" cannot become a hiding place for a genuinely buried button.

- [x] **The coach can change your session volume and your training days, 30 Aug 2026 — audit §2.4.** Both tools had been declared to the model and then declined on every single call since they were written — the coach could discuss cutting Tuesday's work or dropping Thursday and do neither, and the decline pointed people at "the set-count controls on the exercise", which change today's live session only and vanish overnight. **Volume is prescription, so the model no longer picks the size of the change.** It says *lighter* or *heavier*; the app moves one set per exercise inside the generator's own floors and role ceilings, refuses to add work that would push the session past the length you asked for, and never touches a deload week. A change that hits a bound is named on the card ("Tuesday goes from 16 sets to 14; two exercises were left alone — at the minimum") rather than quietly doing less than it claimed. Schedule changes carry the complete new week and rebuild through the same path the Profile screen's rebuild offer already uses. Both ride the propose → confirm → execute → receipt → undo rail: nothing applies without a tap, a double tap cannot apply twice, and past weeks are never rewritten. **Needs a `chat-gemini` deploy — the client and the function must ship together.**

- [x] **THE OLD WRITERS ARE DELETED, NOT LEFT UNREACHABLE.** `adjust_volume` only ever mutated a flat fallback the Exercise tab stopped rendering from, and `update_workout_schedule` PATCHed `fitness_profiles.weekly_schedule` — a column nothing displays — which is how one real profile ended up carrying three mutually disagreeing schedules at once. Both client executors and both action types are gone from `PlanAction`. An unreachable second writer is one restored code path away from being the same bug again.

- [x] **THREE GATES HAD PINNED THESE TOOLS AS "STILL DECLINING", and each carried its own "so this check still means something" guard — which is exactly what went red when the tools were wired.** The lists were correct when written and became claims about the source the source had stopped making. All three now DERIVE the declining set by reading the handlers, so the honesty rule they encode (app-voice copy must never promise a capability the coach refuses) keeps its teeth against whatever declines next, instead of being retired along with the two names it happened to be about.

- [x] **ELEVEN MUTATIONS, AND TWO OF THEM CAUGHT THE GATE RATHER THAN THE CODE.** Removing the client's confirm branch left the gate GREEN — the *undo* branch names the same two kinds, so a check for the bare string was satisfied by a completely different code path. Deleting the deload filter from the proposal also left it green, satisfied by the card's own "your deload week is left alone" note two lines below. Both checks are now anchored to the code that does the work. Same defect shape as the migration that DROPped policy names it had derived instead of read: a check passing on the sentence about the rule rather than the rule.

- [x] **The round timer is now a colour field, 30 Aug 2026 — design handoff "Timer colour states", 2a.** Work floods the Tools tab mint, rest amber, finished red, with dark same-hue ink for the type and controls so contrast holds. Readable across a gym instead of a text colour you had to be holding the phone to see. The dock stays dark and uncovered, so you can walk away mid-round and the timer keeps running. **The handoff said no hook change was needed; two were.** `isRoundComplete` was true for exactly one render before collapsing, so the finished state could not be shown at all — and that same bug is why the old panel's "All rounds complete" line had never once appeared. Round mode also had no pause: `stop()` erases a deadline-anchored round rather than pausing it. Both fixed. Gated by `test:timer-field`, seven mutations each turning it red, plus `npm run render:timer-field` to see all four states at once.

- [x] **A session shorter than the one you asked for now says why, 30 Aug 2026 — audit §6.5.** 2.3% of plans have a training day more than 10% under the length requested, worst case 35 minutes against a 45-60 request. The cause is a thin pool — 5 of 6 are minimalist equipment, 5 of 6 are novice — so the fix is NOT more work: padding a session with repeats to hit a number is volume for the sake of a target. It now explains that the equipment is the limit and points at the add-work button. Deload and low-recovery weeks are left alone, because those are short on purpose. Also fixes a smaller thing found on the way: the "~52 min" chip was a documented prop that nothing ever passed, so it had never rendered. **Not one set, rep or exercise changed** — gated on that specifically.

- [ ] **ASHLEY'S CALL: a low-recovery plan cannot get 15% less work, because there is nowhere left to cut.** Measured: of 45 failing combinations, all 45 have half or more of their exercises already at the 2-set floor. The multiplier is applying correctly; the floor is binding. Options: (a) cut whole exercises once sets bottom out — a real reduction, but it changes weekly training volume; (b) accept the floor and stop counting it as a failure — honest, but the metric then measures something narrower; (c) lower the 2-set floor. Recommend (a) with a plan first, since it changes prescribed volume. Nothing is broken today — a low-recovery person still gets less than a high-recovery one, just not 15% less.

- [x] **You can now sign back in — the half of the account that was missing, 30 Aug 2026.** The email prompt shipped yesterday and on its own did nothing: it let you attach an email, and there was nowhere to use it. Clear your browser or pick up a new phone and the app signed you in anonymously as somebody new, with the email stored and unreachable — promising a recovery it could not perform. There is now a sign-in screen, offered beside onboarding (never in front of it) at the one moment somebody could be looking at a fresh app thinking "but I already have an account".

- [x] **§10 code left behind, triaged rather than bulk-deleted.** Two whole modules gone — the superseded offline session cache and an entire unused hook — plus eight daily-tracking functions whose work moved to the set-log and cardio stores. But the audit's list mixed dead code with **missing features**: there is no undo for deleting a note or a goal, a logged set can be deleted but not edited, and a water log cannot be removed. Deleting those would remove capability the app was built to have and erase the evidence it was intended, so they are recorded as gaps instead. `test:no-dead-code` keeps the distinction honest and holds unreachable functions to a budget. Two of the audit's own entries were already wired up by the time it shipped.
